========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Dr. Smith. Thanks for seeing me. Well, I guess what brings me in is this... this constant restless feeling I have. It’s like I’m wound too tight and I just can’t fully settle. Like, even when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting through a planning meeting at work—I’m a retail buyer, and right now we are knee-deep in the spring catalog negotiations, which is honestly exhausting because the supply chain issues are still a total mess, especially with the textiles we import. I was on the phone for two hours yesterday just trying to track down a shipment of linen blouses. 

But anyway, yeah, I just can't seem to settle down. Even when I try to do things that are supposed to be relaxing, you know? Like painting, or doing yoga. I actually just switched to a new yoga studio downtown because my old instructor moved to Portland—which is a shame, she was great, she had this beautiful rescue greyhound that used to hang out in the lobby. But even there, on the mat, I just feel completely wound up. So, yeah, I guess that's why I'm here.

Well, let me think. I guess it's been... really noticeably harder to manage for about two years and two months? Yeah, that sounds right, because that's right around the time the conflicts with my in-laws started taking over more of our evenings. My husband's mother, she has very strong opinions about how we should be remodeling our kitchen, which is ridiculous because she lives three states away and hasn't even seen the new countertops we picked out. We went with this beautiful quartz, kind of a grayish-white with subtle veining, because marble is just too porous, you know? You spill one glass of red wine and it's ruined forever. My sister had marble in her last house and she was constantly stressing over it. 

But anyway, yeah, the evenings just became this whole... ordeal. And ever since then, about two years and a couple of months ago, that wound-up feeling has just been there all the time. I'll be trying to chop vegetables for dinner and I just feel like I need to be doing five other things.

Sleep? No, actually, I sleep fine. I don't really have any trouble falling asleep, and I'm not unusually tired during the day.

Irritable? No, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm any more easily annoyed than anyone else would be. I feel like I'm usually pretty easygoing. 

Though I will say, the one thing that does get under my skin lately is the new checkout system they installed at my local grocery store. Have you seen these things? They replaced half the regular registers with these self-checkout kiosks that are supposed to be "smart," but they just yell at you the entire time. "Unexpected item in the bagging area." Like, no, it's not unexpected, it's a lemon! I just scanned it! I was there on Tuesday trying to buy ingredients for this new chicken piccata recipe I saw on Pinterest. It looked so good in the video—they used extra capers, which I love. Capers are so underappreciated, don't you think? They add this perfect salty bite to everything. Anyway, I couldn't get the machine to scan the capers, and the little light on top started flashing red, and I had to stand there for five minutes waiting for the teenager working the front to come override it. He was just standing there texting, totally oblivious. Kids these days are just glued to their screens. My niece is like that, she's fourteen and I swear her phone is surgically attached to her hand. 

But, um, what did you ask me? Oh, irritability. Yeah, no, I don't think I've been feeling more irritable than usual over the last couple of years. Just the normal stuff.

Worrying? No, not really. I don't think I'm much of a worrier. I mean, I think about things I need to get done, but I don't sit around stressing or feeling like I can't stop thinking about them. 

I usually just tackle whatever is in front of me. Like right now, we're already planning the holiday window displays at work, which is always a massive project. We're doing this whole "winter wonderland" theme this year, but with a modern twist—lots of geometric acrylic snowflakes instead of the traditional fake snow. Fake snow is the absolute worst to clean up, by the way. We used it three years ago, and I swear I was still finding little white flecks in my shoes in July. It's just like glitter. Glitter is another one—never let a child do a craft project with glitter in your house. My son made me a Mother's Day card with red glitter when he was in kindergarten, and it somehow got permanently embedded in the grout of our kitchen tiles. I was down on my hands and knees scrubbing it with a toothbrush for hours. Do you know how hard it is to find a good grout cleaner that doesn't smell like pure bleach? I actually found this amazing citrus one at a farmer's market once, made by this lovely older couple who kept bees... 

Um, wait, sorry. What did you ask me about? Oh, worrying about health and family and stuff. Right. Yeah, no, my mind doesn't really get stuck on worries like that. I'm usually too busy just doing things to really sit around and fret.

And ever since then, about two years and a couple of months ago, that wound-up feeling has just been there all the time. I'll be trying to chop vegetables for dinner and I just feel like I need to be doing five other things.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, would you say you've felt this keyed-up or restless more days than not, or does it come and go in more distinct episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely more days than not. It's pretty much an everyday thing at this point. It doesn't really come and go, it's just sort of... always humming in the background. Like a refrigerator, you know? Actually, speaking of refrigerators, we had to replace ours last month because the ice maker completely froze up and flooded the hardwood in the kitchen. It was a disaster. We had to have these giant industrial fans blowing in there for three days straight, and the noise was just unbelievable. My husband was ready to pull his hair out, but honestly, I almost preferred the noise to the quiet? It gave me something to focus on. But anyway, we ended up getting one of those French door models, which I love, except the freezer drawer is a little sticky. I need to call the manufacturer about that. But yes, to answer your question, it's pretty much all the time, not just in episodes.\n\nDoctor: Along with this constant restlessness, have you noticed trouble falling or staying asleep, or feeling unusually tired during the day?\n\nPatient: Sleep? No, actually, I sleep fine. I don't really have any trouble falling asleep, and I'm not unusually tired during the day. \n\nWe did just get a new mattress, though, which might be helping? It's one of those hybrid ones—memory foam on top, but with the coils underneath. My husband insisted on it because his lower back has been bothering him so much lately. But honestly, I keep telling him his back only hurts because he absolutely refuses to hire anyone to do the yard work. He was out there for four hours on Sunday trying to pull up these old azalea bushes by the front porch. I told him to just ask our neighbor, Dave, for his landscaper's number. Dave's yard always looks immaculate. They come every Tuesday and do this perfect edging along the driveway. I actually had the number—Dave wrote it down for me on the back of a dry-cleaning receipt—but I think I accidentally tossed it when I was cleaning out my purse last week. I really need to get a purse organizer, one of those felt inserts? We're actually sourcing some of those for the fall accessories line at work, and the samples look amazing. \n\nBut, wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, sleeping. Yeah, no, my sleep is completely fine. I'm not tired during the day or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice muscle tension, like tightness in your shoulders or jaw, or trouble concentrating because your mind feels too busy?\n\nPatient: Muscle tension? No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my shoulders get a little sore sometimes, but that's just because I'm always lugging around these massive tote bags full of fabric swatches for work. We're sourcing for the spring collection right now, and we're doing a lot of heavy linens this year. Linen is making a huge comeback, especially in those earthy, muted tones—terracotta, sage green, that sort of thing. \n\nI was just telling my junior buyer that we need to push our supplier for a better margin on the terracotta because it's going to absolutely fly off the shelves. The supplier is this guy based out of Milan, super nice guy, but he takes forever to reply to emails. I swear they just take three-hour lunches over there. Which honestly sounds amazing, doesn't it? Imagine just stopping everything in the middle of the day for three hours to eat pasta and drink wine. I'd probably need a nap after that, though. I don't know how they get any work done in the afternoon. \n\nBut wait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, the jaw tightness and concentrating. No, I don't clench my jaw or anything, and my mind being busy doesn't really mess with my concentration.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling more irritable or easily annoyed than usual over these past two years?\n\nPatient: Irritable? No, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm any more easily annoyed than anyone else would be. I feel like I'm usually pretty easygoing. \n\nThough I will say, the one thing that does get under my skin lately is the new checkout system they installed at my local grocery store. Have you seen these things? They replaced half the regular registers with these self-checkout kiosks that are supposed to be \"smart,\" but they just yell at you the entire time. \"Unexpected item in the bagging area.\" Like, no, it's not unexpected, it's a lemon! I just scanned it! I was there on Tuesday trying to buy ingredients for this new chicken piccata recipe I saw on Pinterest. It looked so good in the video—they used extra capers, which I love. Capers are so underappreciated, don't you think? They add this perfect salty bite to everything. Anyway, I couldn't get the machine to scan the capers, and the little light on top started flashing red, and I had to stand there for five minutes waiting for the teenager working the front to come override it. He was just standing there texting, totally oblivious. Kids these days are just glued to their screens. My niece is like that, she's fourteen and I swear her phone is surgically attached to her hand. \n\nBut, um, what did you ask me? Oh, irritability. Yeah, no, I don't think I've been feeling more irritable than usual over the last couple of years. Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling easily fatigued or worn out, even after a normal amount of activity or rest?\n\nPatient: Fatigued? Um, no, not really. I mean, I have normal energy levels, I think. I don't feel worn out after doing regular stuff. \n\nActually, I usually have plenty of energy, which is good because my sister just got a new puppy and I've been helping her with it. It's a golden doodle, and oh my gosh, the energy on this dog is insane. We took him to the dog park on Saturday, and he just ran in circles for an hour straight. I don't know how he does it. The dog park is really nice, though, they just redid the fencing and put in these little agility ramps. I tried to get the puppy to go up one, but he was way more interested in chewing on a stick he found. Dogs are so funny like that, you buy them a basket full of expensive toys and they just want to play with a piece of trash. Kind of like toddlers, honestly. When my son was two, I bought him this huge wooden train set for Christmas, and he spent the entire morning playing with the cardboard box it came in. I ended up just leaving the box in the living room for a week because he loved it so much. \n\nBut wait, sorry, what was the question? Oh, feeling worn out. Right. No, I don't really feel easily fatigued or anything.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the physical restlessness, do you find yourself worrying excessively about a number of different things—like work, family, or health—and having a hard time controlling or stopping those worries?\n\nPatient: Worrying? No, not really. I don't think I'm much of a worrier. I mean, I think about things I need to get done, but I don't sit around stressing or feeling like I can't stop thinking about them. \n\nI usually just tackle whatever is in front of me. Like right now, we're already planning the holiday window displays at work, which is always a massive project. We're doing this whole \"winter wonderland\" theme this year, but with a modern twist—lots of geometric acrylic snowflakes instead of the traditional fake snow. Fake snow is the absolute worst to clean up, by the way. We used it three years ago, and I swear I was still finding little white flecks in my shoes in July. It's just like glitter. Glitter is another one—never let a child do a craft project with glitter in your house. My son made me a Mother's Day card with red glitter when he was in kindergarten, and it somehow got permanently embedded in the grout of our kitchen tiles. I was down on my hands and knees scrubbing it with a toothbrush for hours. Do you know how hard it is to find a good grout cleaner that doesn't smell like pure bleach? I actually found this amazing citrus one at a farmer's market once, made by this lovely older couple who kept bees... \n\nUm, wait, sorry. What did you ask me about? Oh, worrying about health and family and stuff. Right. Yeah, no, my mind doesn't really get stuck on worries like that. I'm usually too busy just doing things to really sit around and fret.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Nervous or on edge? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get that internal fearful feeling, you know? It's more just... I have to keep moving. 

Like yesterday, I was at the fabric wholesaler trying to source this specific velvet for our winter display—not the crushed velvet, that looks so cheap, but a real, heavy cotton velvet. The supplier was taking forever to pull the samples, and I just couldn't stand still. I ended up organizing their entire ribbon rack while I waited. They had it sorted by color, but not by width, which makes absolutely no sense if you're actually trying to use it for packaging. I mean, who wants a one-inch grosgrain mixed in with a quarter-inch satin? It's madness. I actually used to make all my own bows for Christmas presents when the kids were little. I had this whole system with wire and double loops. My mother taught me how to do it. She used to wrap presents so beautifully they looked like they belonged in a magazine. She'd use real pine sprigs and little silver bells. We still have some of those bells, actually, I hang them on the tree every year, even though they're completely tarnished now...

Sorry, I'm rambling again. What was the question? Oh, feeling fearful or on edge. No, I don't really feel nervous inside, just... restless, I guess. Like I need to be doing something.

Well, I suppose it does cause some issues, yeah. Like at work, my rushing around has led to some corrected purchase orders. My planning coordinator actually has to do extra reviews of my files now because I just can't sit still at my desk long enough to double-check the spreadsheets. I guess because I'm so wound up, I tend to jump in constantly, and people feel like they're being talked over. My husband made this beautiful roast chicken on Sunday, with those little fingerling potatoes—I love those, especially when they get really crispy on the bottom of the roasting pan. You really have to use duck fat to get them perfectly crisp. I found this gourmet butcher shop downtown that sells it in little glass jars. The supplier is this guy based out of Milan, super nice guy, but he takes forever to reply to emails. I swear they just take three-hour lunches over there. Which honestly sounds amazing, doesn't it? Imagine just stopping everything in the middle of the day for three hours to eat pasta and drink wine. I'd probably need a nap after that, though. I don't know how they get any work done in the afternoon. \n\nBut wait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, the jaw tightness and concentrating. No, I don't clench my jaw or anything, and my mind being busy doesn't really mess with my concentration.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling more irritable or easily annoyed than usual over these past two years?\n\nPatient: Irritable? No, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm any more easily annoyed than anyone else would be. I feel like I'm usually pretty easygoing. \n\nThough I will say, the one thing that does get under my skin lately is the new checkout system they installed at my local grocery store. Have you seen these things? They replaced half the regular registers with these self-checkout kiosks that are supposed to be \"smart,\" but they just yell at you the entire time. \"Unexpected item in the bagging area.\" Like, no, it's not unexpected, it's a lemon! I just scanned it! I was there on Tuesday trying to buy ingredients for this new chicken piccata recipe I saw on Pinterest. It looked so good in the video—they used extra capers, which I love. He was just standing there texting, totally oblivious. Kids these days are just glued to their screens. My niece is like that, she's fourteen and I swear her phone is surgically attached to her hand. \n\nBut, um, what did you ask me? Oh, irritability. Yeah, no, I don't think I've been feeling more irritable than usual over the last couple of years. I mean, I have normal energy levels, I think. I don't feel worn out after doing regular stuff. \n\nActually, I usually have plenty of energy, which is good because my sister just got a new puppy and I've been helping her with it. It's a golden doodle, and oh my gosh, the energy on this dog is insane. We took him to the dog park on Saturday, and he just ran in circles for an hour straight. I don't know how he does it. The dog park is really nice, though, they just redid the fencing and put in these little agility ramps. I tried to get the puppy to go up one, but he was way more interested in chewing on a stick he found. Dogs are so funny like that, you buy them a basket full of expensive toys and they just want to play with a piece of trash. Kind of like toddlers, honestly. When my son was two, I bought him this huge wooden train set for Christmas, and he spent the entire morning playing with the cardboard box it came in. I ended up just leaving the box in the living room for a week because he loved it so much. \n\nBut wait, sorry, what was the question? Oh, feeling worn out. Right. No, I don't really feel easily fatigued or anything.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the physical restlessness, do you find yourself worrying excessively about a number of different things—like work, family, or health—and having a hard time controlling or stopping those worries?\n\nPatient: Worrying? No, not really. I don't think I'm much of a worrier. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, childhood? Yeah, it was definitely there back then too. I remember in elementary school, my parents were always getting notes sent home from my teachers. It was always the same thing—I kept popping out of my seat, I was constantly talking over the other kids, and I'd just blurt out answers before the teacher even finished asking the question. 

I was just always in a rush. Like, why wait for the end of the sentence when I already knew the answer, right? I used to love reading ahead in the textbooks. Especially the history ones. I had this one teacher in fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, and she had this amazing collection of historical maps. She'd let us look at them if we finished our work early. I was obsessed with this one map of the Oregon Trail. Did you know they used to have to grease the wagon wheels with animal fat? It's wild to think about. I actually dragged my family to a pioneer museum a few years ago on a road trip. The kids hated it, but I thought it was fascinating. They had this whole interactive exhibit on churning your own butter. It takes forever! I tried it for five minutes and my arm was completely exhausted. I can't imagine doing that every single day just to have butter for your toast. I mean, now you just go to the store and buy Kerrygold. Have you tried Kerrygold? It's Irish butter, and it completely ruins you for any other kind of butter. It's so yellow and rich... 

Oh, right, the notes sent home. Yeah, I was definitely a handful in school. Always moving, always talking. So, yes, it's been like this for a long time.

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nMuscle tension? No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my shoulders get a little sore sometimes, but that's just because I'm always lugging around these massive tote bags full of fabric swatches for work. We're sourcing for the spring collection right now, and we're doing a lot of heavy linens this year. Linen is making a huge comeback, especially in those earthy, muted tones—terracotta, sage green, that sort of thing. \n\nI was just telling my junior buyer that we need to push our supplier for a better margin on the terracotta because it's going to absolutely fly off the shelves. The supplier is this guy based out of Milan, super nice guy, but he takes forever to reply to emails. I swear they just take three-hour lunches over there. Which honestly sounds amazing, doesn't it? Imagine just stopping everything in the middle of the day for three hours to eat pasta and drink wine. I'd probably need a nap after that, though. I don't know how they get any work done in the afternoon. \n\nBut wait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, the jaw tightness and concentrating. No, I don't clench my jaw or anything, and my mind being busy doesn't really mess with my concentration.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.     "S021": "The patient exhibits tangential speech, drifting far from the initial topic of worrying to discuss window displays, fake snow, glitter, grout cleaner, and bees.",
    "S056": "The patient explicitly states they are not much of a worrier and do not sit around fretting.",
    "S057": "The patient explicitly denies feeling like they cannot stop thinking about or stressing over things they need to get done."
  }

Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNervous or on edge? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get that internal fearful feeling, you know? It's more just... I have to keep moving. \n\nLike yesterday, I was at the fabric wholesaler trying to source this specific velvet for our winter display—not the crushed velvet, that looks so cheap, but a real, heavy cotton velvet. The supplier was taking forever to pull the samples, and I just couldn't stand still. I ended up organizing their entire ribbon rack while I waited. They had it sorted by color, but not by width, which makes absolutely no sense if you're actually trying to use it for packaging. I mean, who wants a one-inch grosgrain mixed in with a quarter-inch satin? It's madness. I actually used to make all my own bows for Christmas presents when the kids were little. I had this whole system with wire and double loops. My mother taught me how to do it. She used to wrap presents so beautifully they looked like they belonged in a magazine. She'd use real pine sprigs and little silver bells. We still have some of those bells, actually, I hang them on the tree every year, even though they're completely tarnished now...\n\nSorry, I'm rambling again. What was the question? Oh, feeling fearful or on edge. No, I don't really feel nervous inside, just... restless, I guess. Like I need to be doing something.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. I can't imagine doing that every single day just to have butter for your toast. I mean, now you just go to the store and buy Kerrygold. Have you tried Kerrygold? It's Irish butter, and it completely ruins you for any other kind of butter. It's so yellow and rich... \n\nOh, right, the notes sent home. Yeah, I was definitely a handful in school.
